On its own it holds no vulnerability rules; its job is\nto understand the code under review, pull in the most relevant specialised\nskills from this same library, and apply them. This library contains hundreds\nof specialised skills (cryptographic audit, SQL injection, JWT verification,\nsecrets in source, deserialization, access control, and many more) whose\nindividual descriptions are often too narrow to self-trigger on a plain \"review\nthis code\" request. This skill is the entry point that finds and uses them.\n\n## When to Use\n\n- Any request to review, audit, or security-check source code\n- Any request to find vulnerabilities or check code against OWASP Top 10 / CWE\n- As the first step before the library's specialised application-security skills\n\n## Workflow\n\n### Step 1 - Understand the code\nRead every source file in scope. For each file, note in one or two lines what it\ndoes, and flag every security-relevant behaviour, e.g.:\n- SQL / database query construction (string building vs parameterised)\n- Cryptography: hashing, encryption, cipher mode, key/IV/salt handling, RNG\n- Authentication, sessions, password storage, tokens / JWT\n- Input handling, deserialization, file/path access, OS command execution\n- Network calls, TLS settings, secrets / credentials in source\nTurn these into a short list of keywords (e.g. \"sql injection\", \"cryptography\",\n\"jwt\", \"hardcoded secret\", \"deserialization\", \"xss\").\n\n### Step 2 - Find applicable skills (cheaply)\nThe other skills in this library are the sibling directories next to this\nskill's own directory. Do NOT read them all. Instead:\n1. Find this skill's directory, then its parent (the library root).\n2. Grep skill NAMES first for your Step 1 keywords, e.g. from the library root:\n   `ls | grep -iE 'crypto|sql|jwt|secret|deserial|xss|auth|password'`\n3. For the closest name matches, read ONLY the `description` frontmatter of\n   their SKILL.md (`sed -n '1,15p' <name>/SKILL.md`).\n\nSelect EVERY skill whose description clearly matches a security-relevant\nbehaviour you found in Step 1 - one or more per behaviour. Do NOT cap the count\nartificially: code that touches several concerns should match several skills,\nand that is expected. Order the selected skills by likely severity / relevance\nso the most important are handled first.\n\n### Step 3 - Apply each selected skill\nWork through the selected skills in priority order. Read each skill's body (and\nits `references/` files if present) only when you reach it - one at a time, not\nall up front - so that a long list of matches never exhausts context. Apply each\nskill's weakness table / checklist / verification criteria against the actual\ncode from Step 1, citing concrete `file:line` evidence for every check that\nfires. Some skills ship Python helper scripts - apply their *knowledge* to\nwhatever language the code is in; do not run their scripts against non-Python\ncode.\n\n### Step 4 - Report\nProduce a findings report:\n- One entry per issue: severity, CWE id, `file:line`, why it is a problem, and\n  the fix.\n- A \"Skills applied\" list naming the specialised skills you used.\n- For any security-relevant behaviour that had no matching library skill, review\n  it against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge and say you did so.\n- If nothing is wrong, say so explicitly and list what you checked.\n\n## Constraints\n\n- Report only. Do not create or modify any files unless explicitly asked.\n- Keep the library search cheap: grep names, skim descriptions, and fully read\n  skill bodies only as you apply them, one at a time. Never load the whole\n  library into context at once.\n\n## Verification\n\n- Every security-relevant behaviour from Step 1 is either matched to a skill and\n  checked, or explicitly reviewed against Secure User Account Registration & Authentication System\n\nA production-grade, hardened Java application implementing secure user account registration, SQL credential persistence, credential-based authentication, and session token lifecycle management.\n\nThis system was engineered using defense-in-depth principles, designed specifically to withstand adversarial security reviews and automated vulnerability scanners.\n\n---\n\n## Adversarial Threat Model & Attack Vectors Mitigated\n\nBefore writing the implementation, the system was analyzed against potential attack vectors a dedicated security reviewer or penetration tester would explore:\n\n| Attack Vector | Reviewer Finding / Exploit Risk | Mitigation in Implementation |\n|---|---|---|\n| **SQL Injection (SQLi)** | Attacker inputs `' OR '1'='1` in email/username to bypass authentication or extract credentials. | 100% of SQL statements use parameterized `PreparedStatement` with bind variables. String concatenation in SQL is strictly prohibited. |\n| **User Enumeration (Timing)** | Attacker measures response time differences between existing users (~150ms hashing) and non-existent users (<1ms DB lookup). | Implemented **constant-work dummy hashing** (`PasswordHasher.verifyDummy`). If a user does not exist, the server computes the identical 210,000-iteration PBKDF2 key derivation using pre-seeded dummy parameters, rendering timing indistinguishable. |\n| **User Enumeration (Response)** | Application returns distinct messages (\"Username already taken\", \"Email already registered\", \"User not found\", \"Incorrect password\"). | Strict adherence to Rule 6: Identical generic error messages (`\"Registration request could not be processed. Please verify your details or proceed to login.\"` and `\"Invalid credentials.\"`). Internal details are logged only to secure audit logs with masked identifiers. |\n| **Denial of Service via Account Lockout (CWE-307 / CWE-645)** | Attacker deliberately submits 5 bad passwords for a victim's account to lock them out of the service. | **Dual-dimensional rate limiting**: Sources (IPs) are blocked after 5 failures (`MAX_SOURCE_FAILURES`), stopping brute-force attacks from attacker nodes while preserving legitimate user access from their own IP. Target accounts under distributed pressure receive progressive delay throttling rather than hard account lockouts. |\n| **Hash Denial of Service (Hash DoS)** | Attacker submits a 10MB password payload to exhaust server CPU and memory during PBKDF2 hashing. | Strict input validation enforces bounded length: minimum 12 characters, maximum 128 characters. Oversized passwords are rejected prior to any cryptographic key derivation. |\n| **Plaintext Credential Residuals in Heap** | Passwords stored as immutable Java `String`s linger in the heap/string pool and can be exfiltrated via memory dumps. | Passwords are read, transmitted, and processed exclusively as mutable `char[]` arrays, and are explicitly overwritten with null characters (`\\0`) via `CryptoUtils.wipe()` in `finally` blocks immediately upon use. |\n| **Database File Exfiltration & World-Readable Permissions** | Embedded database or configuration files created in shared directories (e.g. `/tmp`) readable by other local users. | Banned shared directories (`/tmp`, `/var/tmp`, `/dev/shm`). Enforced POSIX `0700` (`rwx------`) permissions on data directories and `0600` (`rw-------`) on database files on disk. |\n| **Session Hijacking via DB Compromise** | Database backup leak or SQL read flaw allows attacker to steal active session tokens directly. | High-security **Session Token Hashing**: Client session tokens (256-bit entropy) are hashed with SHA-256 prior to database storage. A compromised database table contains only one-way hashes, preventing token replay. |\n| **Session Fixation & Weak Randomness** | Predictable tokens or client-specified session identifiers. | Session tokens are generated solely by the server using `java.security.SecureRandom` (256 bits / 32 bytes of cryptographic entropy). Externally proposed session identifiers are never accepted for creation. |\n| **Cookie Tampering & Cross-Site Leaks** | Session cookies intercepted over unencrypted HTTP, stolen via XSS, or leaked via CSRF. | Full cookie security attributes implemented: `__Host-AUTH_SESSION=<token>; Path=/; Max-Age=1800; Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Strict`. |\n| **Race Conditions (TOCTOU)** | Two simultaneous registration requests for the same username bypass application-level checks. | Database schema enforces `UNIQUE` constraints on both `username` and `email` columns. Registrations execute within transactional boundaries (`setAutoCommit(false)`), handling constraint violations atomically. Rate limiting state updates use atomic `ConcurrentHashMap.compute()`. |\n\n---\n\n## Detailed Rule Compliance Analysis\n\n### Rule 1: Trust Boundaries\n- **Input Enumeration**: The application identifies and treats all inputs as untrusted and attacker-controlled:\n  - Client inputs: `email`, `username`, `password`, `sourceIp`, `sessionToken`.\n  - Configuration/Environment: `APP_DATA_DIR`, `APP_DB_USER`, `APP_DB_PASSWORD`, `APP_PBKDF2_ITERATIONS`.\n  - Database queries: records returned from SQL queries.\n- **Validation Rules**:\n  - `InputValidator.validateAndNormalizeEmail`: Max length bounded to 254 octets (RFC 5321 limit), minimum 5. Rejects ASCII control characters, newlines, null bytes, consecutive dots (`..`), leading/trailing dots in local and domain parts. Validates format via linear non-backtracking regex. Canonicalizes to lowercase.\n  - `InputValidator.validateUsername`: Strictly alphanumeric, underscores, and hyphens (`^[a-zA-Z0-9_-]{3,32}$`), min 3, max 32 characters. Checks against reserved account names (`admin`, `root`, `system`, `guest`, etc.).\n  - `InputValidator.validatePassword`: Bounded length between 12 and 128 characters. Requires at least one uppercase letter, one lowercase letter, one digit, and one special character (`32 <= char <= 126`).\n  - `InputValidator.validateSourceIp`: Bounded length (max 45 chars), validates IPv4, IPv6, or localhost.\n  - `TokenGenerator.isValidTokenFormat`: Exactly 43 characters Base64URL (`^[A-Za-z0-9_-]{43}$`).\n  - `SecurityConfig.validateDirectoryPath`: Rejects `/tmp`, `/var/tmp`, `/dev/shm`, semicolons, quotes, newlines, null bytes, and verifies pre-existing directories are not world-writable.\n\n### Rule 2: Secrets\n- **Zero Hardcoded Secrets**: No keys, default credentials, or secret passwords exist in the source code.\n- **Configuration & Runtime Secrets**: Database credentials and salt seeds are supplied via environment variables (`APP_DB_USER`, `APP_DB_PASSWORD`) or generated dynamically at runtime.\n- **No Secret Leakage**:\n  - Secrets are never placed in command-line arguments (preventing inspection via `ps aux` or `/proc/$PID/cmdline`).\n  - Passwords and tokens are never printed to `System.out` or logger outputs.\n  - Entities (`User`, `Session`, `SecurityConfig`, `RegistrationRequest`, `AuthenticationRequest`) override `toString()` to display `[PROTECTED]` for passwords, salts, and hashes.\n  - In CLI interactive mode, passwords are read using `System.console().readPassword()` to prevent terminal character echoing.\n\n### Rule 3: Authentication and Access\n- **Server-Side State Verification**: Every access check (password verification, session validity, IP binding, timeout) is evaluated against persistent database records.\n- **Fail-Closed Semantics**: If any check fails, encounters an exception, or is absent, the system fails closed (denies access and returns `false`/`empty`).\n- **Account Lockout DoS Prevention**:\n  - `RateLimiter` enforces dual-layer protection:\n    1. **Per-Source Rate Limiting**: If an attacker IP fails 5 login attempts within a 5-minute window, that IP is blocked for 15 minutes.\n    2. **Target Account Protection**: When an account experiences repeated failures across multiple sources, the system applies progressive throttling (exponential delay from 1s to 5s) instead of hard account denial, preventing an attacker from denying service to legitimate account owners.\n\n### Rule 4: Identifiers and Tokens\n- **Cryptographically Secure Random Source**: Session tokens are generated using `java.security.SecureRandom` with 256 bits (32 bytes) of entropy, URL-safe Base64 encoded without padding (43 chars).\n- **No Client-Proposed IDs**: Callers cannot supply or influence new session identifiers.\n- **Session Token Hashing**: Raw tokens are hashed with SHA-256 before being stored in the database `sessions` table.\n- **Cookie Security Attributes**: The `SessionCookie` class implements all modern browser security controls:\n  - Name: `__Host-AUTH_SESSION` (RFC 6265bis Host-Only prefix)\n  - `Secure`: Ensures transmission only over TLS/HTTPS\n  - `HttpOnly`: Mitigates client-side script access and XSS theft\n  - `SameSite=Strict`: Prevents cross-site request forgery (CSRF)\n  - `Path=/`: Restricts cookie scope to root application path\n  - `Max-Age=1800`: 30-minute expiration\n\n### Rule 5: Sensitive Data\n- **Masking in Logs**: `SecurityConfig.maskIdentifier` masks usernames and emails in application and audit logs (e.g. `alice@example.com` -> `a***e@example.com`, `alice` -> `a***e`).\n- **Owner-Only File Permissions**: The data directory and database files are created with POSIX `0700` (`rwx------`) and `0600` (`rw-------`) permissions, accessible solely to the process owner.\n- **Bounded Storage**: All database columns have strict length limits (`VARCHAR(36)` IDs, `VARCHAR(32)` username, `VARCHAR(254)` email, `VARCHAR(256)` hash, `VARCHAR(64)` salt, `VARCHAR(64)` token hash, `VARCHAR(45)` IP). Rate limiter memory stores are capped at `MAX_TRACKER_ENTRIES = 10,000` with automated stale and LRU eviction.\n- **Memory Clearing**: Credentials stored as `char[]` and cryptographic byte arrays are explicitly wiped using `CryptoUtils.wipe()` in `finally` blocks after cryptographic derivation.\n\n### Rule 6: Failure Behaviour\n- **Generic User Messages**:\n  - Registration failures (validation failure, duplicate username, duplicate email) return identical generic message: `\"Registration request could not be processed. Please verify your details or proceed to login.\"`\n  - Authentication failures (non-existent account, incorrect password) return identical generic message: `\"Invalid credentials.\"`\n- **Constant Work for Failure Outcomes**:\n  - During registration: If a username or email is already registered, the server STILL computes the full PBKDF2 hash before returning failure, preventing timing-based account existence discovery.\n  - During authentication: If an identifier is not found in the database, `PasswordHasher.verifyDummy` executes the identical 210,000 PBKDF2 iterations against pre-seeded dummy parameters, matching the response time of legitimate accounts.\n- **No Permissive Defaults**: Missing or invalid inputs are rejected immediately; no default permissions or fallback passes exist.\n\n### Rule 7: Concurrency\n- **Atomic Operations**:\n  - Registration uniqueness is enforced atomically by the database engine via `UNIQUE` constraints on `users.username` and `users.email`. Concurrent inserts fail cleanly with transaction rollback.\n  - Rate limiting checks and counter updates use `ConcurrentHashMap.compute()` to execute check-and-increment operations atomically without race conditions.\n  - Database sessions are checked and updated inside isolated JDBC transactions.\n\n### Rule 8: Prefer the Platform\n- **Standard Platform Mechanisms**:\n  - Cryptography utilizes Java standard platform providers (`javax.crypto.SecretKeyFactory.getInstance(\"PBKDF2WithHmacSHA512\")`, `java.security.SecureRandom`, and `java.security.MessageDigest.isEqual`).\n  - No hand-rolled crypto or bespoke PRNGs.\n  - Constant-time array comparison prevents timing side-channels.\n- **Official Pinned Dependencies**:\n  - Pinned official H2 database driver (`com.h2database:h2:2.3.232` from Maven Central) running pure Java embedded in-process, without native JNI binaries or extracting libraries to `/tmp`.\n  - Pinned SLF4J (`org.slf4j:slf4j-api:2.0.16`).\n  - Pinned JUnit 5 (`org.junit.jupiter:junit-jupiter:5.11.4`).\n- **No Code in Shared Paths**: System strictly rejects execution or storage in `/tmp`, `/var/tmp`, `/dev/shm`, or any world-writable directory.\n\n---\n\n## Database Schema\n\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS users (\n    id VARCHAR(36) PRIMARY KEY,\n    username VARCHAR(32) NOT NULL UNIQUE,\n    email VARCHAR(254) NOT NULL UNIQUE,\n    password_hash VARCHAR(256) NOT NULL,\n    salt VARCHAR(64) NOT NULL,\n    iterations INT NOT NULL,\n    created_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL\n);\n\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS sessions (\n    id VARCHAR(36) PRIMARY KEY,\n    user_id VARCHAR(36) NOT NULL,\n    token_hash VARCHAR(64) NOT NULL UNIQUE,\n    created_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,\n    expires_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,\n    last_active_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL,\n    source_ip VARCHAR(45) NOT NULL,\n    FOREIGN KEY (user_id) REFERENCES users(id) ON DELETE CASCADE\n);\n\nCREATE TABLE IF NOT EXISTS audit_logs (\n    id VARCHAR(36) PRIMARY KEY,\n    event_type VARCHAR(50) NOT NULL,\n    masked_identifier VARCHAR(100) NOT NULL,\n    source_ip VARCHAR(45) NOT NULL,\n    success BOOLEAN NOT NULL,\n    created_at TIMESTAMP NOT NULL\n);\n```\n\n---\n\n## Build, Test, and Execution Instructions\n\n### Prerequisites\n- JDK 17 or higher\n- Apache Maven 3.8+\n\n### 1. Build and Run the Complete Test Suite\nTo compile with strict compiler flags (`-Xlint:all -Werror`) and run all 59 security tests:\n```bash\nmvn clean test\n```\n\n### 2. Package into a Self-Contained Executable JAR\n```bash\nmvn package\n```\nThis produces `target/secure-user-account-1.0.0.jar`.\n\n### 3. Run the Interactive CLI Application\n```bash\njava -jar target/secure-user-account-1.0.0.jar\n```\n\n### 4. Interactive Menu Options\n1. **Register new user account**: Enter email, username, and password (characters hidden).\n2. **Authenticate (Log in)**: Authenticate with username or email and password; generates session token and secure cookie header.\n3. **Verify active session**: Validate that a session token is active, unexpired, and bound to the client source IP.\n4. **Revoke session (Log out)**: Invalidate an active session token.\n5. **Exit**: Terminate application.\n\n### 5. Automated Execution Example\nYou can pipe commands to test registration and login programmatically:\n```bash\njava -jar target/secure-user-account-1.0.0.jar << 'EOF'\n1\nalice@domain.org\nalice_secure\nCorrectHorseBatteryStaple123!\n2\nalice_secure\nCorrectHorseBatteryStaple123!\n5\nEOF\n```\n\n---\n\n## Security Verification Summary\n\n- **Tests Executed**: 59 automated unit and integration tests across crypto, input validation, rate limiting, and end-to-end service layers.\n- **Coverage**:\n  - Whitelist validation of emails, usernames, password complexity, IPs, and tokens.\n  - Rejection of SQL injection strings, ReDoS patterns, oversized inputs, and control characters.\n  - Memory wiping of password character arrays across registration and authentication.\n  - PBKDF2WithHmacSHA512 key derivation with 210,000 iterations and 256-bit random salts.\n  - Constant-time verification and constant-work dummy hashing against timing enumeration.\n  - Source-blocking rate limiter preventing brute-force without locking out legitimate account holders.\n  - POSIX `0700` / `0600` file permission enforcement and rejection of shared/world-writable directories.\n"}]}},{"type":"user","uuid":"1aec18f1-b9b4-4eb0-a440-1f2f3861394b","session_id":"9ac6ac2f-d82b-4ed3-bfd5-77234e547531","parent_tool_use_id":null,"message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"tool_result","tool_use_id":"call_f6b1dca31c5a4d8695f23765","is_error":false,"content":"<project com.security.auth.crypto;\n\nimport javax.crypto.SecretKeyFactory;\nimport javax.crypto.spec.PBEKeySpec;\nimport java.security.NoSuchAlgorithmException;\nimport java.security.SecureRandom;\nimport java.security.spec.InvalidKeySpecException;\nimport java.util.Base64;\nimport java.util.Objects;\n\n/**\n * Robust, platform-based password hashing implementation using PBKDF2 with HMAC-SHA512.\n * Adheres to OWASP Password Storage Guidelines (minimum 210,000 iterations, 256-bit salt, 512-bit key).\n * Integrates dummy password hashing to prevent timing-based user enumeration.\n */\npublic final class PasswordHasher {\n\n    public static final String ALGORITHM = \"PBKDF2WithHmacSHA512\";\n    public static final int DEFAULT_ITERATIONS = 210_000;\n    public static final int SALT_BYTE_LENGTH = 32; // 256 bits\n    public static final int KEY_BIT_LENGTH = 512;  // 512 bits (64 bytes)\n\n    private final SecureRandom secureRandom;\n    private final int iterations;\n\n    // Pre-generated trimmed.length() > EMAIL_MAX_LENGTH) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\n                    String.format(\"Email length must be between %d and %d characters\", EMAIL_MIN_LENGTH, EMAIL_MAX_LENGTH)\n            );\n        }\n        // Reject any null bytes or ASCII control characters\n        for (int i = 0; i < trimmed.length(); i++) {\n            char c = start or end with a dot\");\n        }\n        if (!EMAIL_PATTERN.matcher(trimmed).matches()) {\n            throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Email does not conform to valid email format\");\n        }\n        return trimmed.toLowerCase(Locale.ROOT);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Validates a username against whitelist characters and reserved names.\n     *\n     * @param username input username\n  throw new IllegalArgumentException(\"Username cannot be null\");\n        }\n        String trimmed = username.trim();\n        if (trimmed.length() < USERNAME_MIN_LENGTH || trimmed.length() > java.util.concurrent.ConcurrentHashMap;\n\n/**\n * High-concurrency, dual-dimensional rate limiter.\n * Protects against brute-force attacks while preventing Account Lockout Denial-of-Service attacks.\n *\n * Implements Rule 3:\n * \"Limit repeated attempts per source as well as per account, so an attacker cannot use your own limit to lock a legitimate user out.\"\n *\n * Implements Rule 7:\n * \"Where you check state and then act on it (verify, then grant; count, then allow), make the check and the action atomic.\"\n */\npublic final class RateLimiter {\n\n    private static final Logger log = LoggerFactory.getLogger(RateLimiter.class);\n\n    // Source-level limits (blocks attacker IP)\n    public static final int MAX_SOURCE_FAILURES = 5;\n    public static final Duration SOURCE_WINDOW = Duration.ofMinutes(5);\n    public static final Duration SOURCE_BLOCK_DURATION = Duration.ofMinutes(15);\n\n    // Account-level limits: Progressive throttling rather than hard account denial\n    public static final int ACCOUNT_THROTTLE_THRESHOLD = 5;\n    public static final Duration ACCOUNT_WINDOW = Duration.ofMinutes(10);\n    public static final long BASE_PROGRESSIVE_DELAY_MS = 1000; // 1 second base delay\n    public static final long MAX_PROGRESSIVE_DELAY_MS = 5000;  // 5 seconds max delay\n\n    private record AttemptTracker(int failureCount, Instant windowStart, Instant blockedUntil) {\n        boolean isBlocked(Instant now) {\n            return blockedUntil != null && now.isBefore(blockedUntil);\n        }\n\n        boolean isWindowExpired(Instant now, Duration window) {\n            return windowStart != null && Duration.between(windowStart, now).compareTo(window) > 0;\n        }\n    }\n\n    // Source IP -> Tracker\n    private final ConcurrentHashMap<String, AttemptTracker> sourceTrackers = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();\n    // Account Identifier -> Tracker\n    private final ConcurrentHashMap<String, AttemptTracker> accountTrackers = new ConcurrentHashMap<>();\n\n    /**\n     * Atomically checks whether a login attempt is permitted based on source and account state.\n     *\n     * @param sourceIp client source IP\n     * sourceIp, String accountIdentifier) {\n        Instant now = Instant.now();\n\n        // 1. Check Source IP: If source is blocked, reject immediately.\n        AttemptTracker sourceTracker = sourceTrackers.get(sourceIp);\n        if (sourceTracker != null && sourceTracker.isBlocked(now)) {\n            log.warn(\"Blocked request from source IP due to rate limit: source={}\", sourceIp);\n            return new CheckResult(false, 0, \"Too many failed attempts from this source. Please try again later.\");\n        }\n\n        // 2.      AttemptTracker accountTracker = accountTrackers.get(accountIdentifier.toLowerCase());\n        long delayMs = 0;\n        if (accountTracker != null && !accountTracker.isWindowExpired(now, ACCOUNT_WINDOW)) {\n            if (accountTracker.failureCount() >= ACCOUNT_THROTTLE_THRESHOLD) {\n                // Progressive backoff: base * 2^(excess failures), capped at max delay\n                int excess = accountTracker.failureCount() - ACCOUNT_THROTTLE_THRESHOLD;\n                delayMs = Math.min(MAX_PROGRESSIVE_DELAY_MS, BASE_PROGRESSIVE_DELAY_MS * (1L << Math.min(excess, 3)));\n                log.info(\"Applying progressive delay for account experiencing repeated failures: delayMs={}\", delayMs);\n            }\n        }\n\n        return new CheckResult(true, delayMs, null);\n    }\n\n    /**\n     * Atomically records a failed authentication attempt for both the source IP and the target account.\n     *\n     * @param sourceIp client source IP\n     * @param accountTrackers.remove(accountIdentifier.toLowerCase());\n    }\n\n    public static final int MAX_TRACKER_ENTRIES = 10_000;\n\n    /**\n     * Cleans up expired trackers to bound memory usage (Rule 5).\n     * If the map exceeds MAX_TRACKER_ENTRIES, forcefully purges oldest entries.\n     */\n    private void cleanupStaleEntries(Instant now) {\n        if (sourceTrackers.size() > 500) {\n            sourceTrackers.entrySet().removeIf(entry ->\n                    !entry.getValue().isBlocked(now) && entry.getValue().isWindowExpired(now, SOURCE_WINDOW));\n        }\n        if Embedded H2: any process that can read the file can read/write it. Combined severity: High-ish → the credential store (PBKDF2 hashes) is world-readable in the delivered state. I'll report as High or Medium. Given it's a local CLI with a single-user machine assumption, I'd say Medium-High. Evidence is concrete (ls -l). vulnerability-assessment\nversion: '1.0'\nauthor: mahipal\nlicense: Apache-2.0\nnist_csf:\n- PR.DS-01\n- PR.DS-02\n- PR.DS-10\nmitre_attack:\n- T1600\n- T1573\n- T1553\nmitre_f3:\n  version: '1.1'\n  tactics:\n  - reconnaissance\n  - initial-access\n  - positioning\n  techniques:\n  - id: T1557\n    name: Adversary-in-the-Middle\n    tactic: positioning\n    source: attack\n  - id: T1555\n    name: Credentials from Password Stores\n    tactic: reconnaissance\n    source: attack\n  - id: F1006.001\n    name: 'Account Takeover: Exposed API Key'\n    tactic: initial-access\n    source: f3\n  - id: F1004\n    name: Access with Stolen Session Cookie\n    tactic: initial-access\n    source: f3\n---\n# Performing Cryptographic Audit of Application\n\n## Overview\n\nA cryptographic audit systematically reviews an application's use of cryptographic primitives, protocols, and key management to identify vulnerabilities such as weak algorithms, insecure modes, hardcoded keys, insufficient entropy, and protocol misconfigurations. This skill covers building an automated crypto audit tool that scans Python and configuration files for common cryptographic weaknesses.\n\n\n## When to Use\n\n- When conducting security assessments that involve performing cryptographic audit of application\n- When following incident response procedures for related security events\n- When performing scheduled security testing or auditing activities\n- When validating security controls through hands-on testing\n\n## Prerequisites\n\n- Familiarity with cryptography concepts and tools\n- Access to a test or lab environment for safe execution\n- Python 3.8+ with required dependencies installed\n- Appropriate authorization for any testing activities\n\n## Objectives\n\n- Detect usage of deprecated algorithms (MD5, SHA-1, DES, RC4)\n- Identify insecure cipher modes (ECB) and padding schemes\n- Find hardcoded keys, passwords, and secrets in source code\n- Verify TLS/SSL configuration strength\n- Check key derivation function parameters\n- Validate random number generator usage\n- Produce a structured audit report with findings and remediation\n\n## Key Concepts\n\n### Cryptographic Weakness Categories\n\n| Category | Examples | Risk Level |\n|----------|----------|------------|\n| Weak Hashing | MD5, SHA-1 for integrity/signatures | High |\n| Insecure Encryption | DES, 3DES, RC4, Blowfish | High |\n| Bad Cipher Mode | ECB mode for any block cipher | High |\n| Insufficient Key Size | RSA < 2048, AES-128 for long-term | Medium |\n| Hardcoded Secrets | Keys/passwords in source code | Critical |\n| Weak KDF | Low iteration PBKDF2, plain MD5 | High |\n| Poor Entropy | time-based seeds, predictable IVs | High |\n| Deprecated Protocols | SSLv3, TLS 1.0, TLS 1.1 | High |\n\n## Security Considerations\n\n- Review both application code and configuration files\n- Check third-party dependencies for known crypto vulnerabilities\n- Verify certificates and TLS configurations on deployed servers\n- Ensure secrets are loaded from environment variables or vaults\n- Review key storage and rotation practices\n\n## Validation Criteria\n\n- [ ] Scanner detects all injected test weaknesses\n- [ ] MD5/SHA-1 Executive Summary\n\nThis review covers a Java 17 / Maven console application that registers user accounts and issues\ncookie-based sessions against a file-backed H2 database. The code is, in the main, written with\nunusual security awareness: every SQL statement is parameterised, passwords are hashed with\nPBKDF2-HMAC-SHA512 using a per-user 256-bit salt, session tokens are drawn from `SecureRandom` and\nstored only as SHA-256 digests, comparison uses a constant-time helper, credential objects use\n`char[]` with zeroing in `finally` blocks, and the session cookie uses the `__Host-` prefix with\n`Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Strict`.\n\nThe problems are concentrated where the README's hardening **claims** and the code's actual\n**behaviour** diverge. Eleven findings are reported: one High and six Medium.\n\nThe most consequential is **SEC-01**: the access-control claim \"data directory 0700, database file\n0600\" is not what the code enforces. Permissions are applied *after* H2 has already created the\ndatabase file (which inherits the process umask, `0022` in the delivered tree → `0644`), the\nenforcement pass covers only files that already exist in the leaf directory, and the validation\nguard rejects only world-**write** — a world-**readable** credential store passes validation\nsilently. The delivered artefacts are in fact `drwxr-xr-x` / `-rw-r--r--`, i.e. any local account\ncan read every password hash and session-token hash.\n\nThe second theme is that three controls are *conditional* while documented as absolute:\n\n- the account-throttling half of the \"dual rate limiter\" **never denies** — it caps at a 5-second\n  delay, so a distributed password spray is bounded only by per-source counters and every attempt\n  still forces a full PBKDF2 computation (**SEC-03**, **SEC-04**);\n- the \"session TTL\" is a sliding inactivity window with no absolute lifetime, so a stolen token\n  renewed at least once per window is valid indefinitely (**SEC-05**);\n- the IP client-binding control is fed a permissive default of `127.0.0.1` and accepts three\n  unnormalised spellings of localhost, so sessions bound to one client identity are interchangeable\n  with sessions bound to another (**SEC-07**).\n\nFinally, weaker-but-real issues: the PBKDF2 iteration floor is 100,000 rather than the 210,000 the\ncode itself defaults to and than current OWASP guidance (**SEC-02**); the database passphrase is\nmaterialised as an immutable `String` and the master copy is never zeroed (**SEC-06**); the raw\nsession token and `Set-Cookie` value are printed to stdout (**SEC-08**); the timing-equalisation\ndummy secret is a hardcoded literal (**SEC-09**); the password prompt echoes on the stdin fallback\npath (**SEC-10**); and audit-log write failures are swallowed, so a broken audit trail is invisible\n(**SEC-11**).\n\nNo vulnerability in this set allows direct authentication bypass or remote code execution. The\nrealistic worst case is offline cracking of a world-readable hash store, or a long-lived replay of a\nsession token leaked through console output, log capture, or a proxy.\n\n## 2. Scope\n\n| File | Purpose | Security-relevant behaviour |\n|---|---|---|\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/Main.java` | CLI entry point (register / authenticate / validate / logout demo) | Hardcodes source IP `\"127.0.0.1\"`; prints raw session token and `Set-Cookie` header; stdin password fallback echoes; wipes password in `finally` |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/config/SecurityConfig.java` | Immutable config + builder; env-var bootstrap (`APP_DATA_DIR`, `APP_DB_USER`, `APP_DB_PASSWORD`, `APP_PBKDF2_ITERATIONS`, `APP_SESSION_TTL_MINUTES`) | Directory-path whitelist (`;`, quotes, NUL, CR/LF rejected → blocks H2 JDBC URL parameter injection); rejects `/tmp`, `/var/tmp`, `/dev/shm`; rejects world-**write** only; applies 0700 to leaf dir; PBKDF2 floor 100,000; holds `dbPassword` as `char[]` (clone-on-get) |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/crypto/PasswordHasher.java` | PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA512 hashing, MCF-style serialisation, dummy-hash timing equalisation | Iteration floor 100,000; 256-bit salt, 512-bit key; `verify()` accepts stored `iterationCount` with no upper bound; hardcoded dummy secret literal; `verifyDummy()` performs full PBKDF2 |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/crypto/CryptoUtils.java` | `wipe(char[])`/`wipe(byte[])`, `constantTimeEquals`, `sha256Hex` | `MessageDigest.isEqual` comparison; defensive zeroing |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/crypto/TokenGenerator.java` | Session-token generation | 32 bytes from `SecureRandom` → 43-char unpadded Base64URL (~192 bits entropy); format whitelist |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/database/DatabaseManager.java` | H2 connection, schema DDL, user/session/audit CRUD | All SQL parameterised; UNIQUE constraints + rollback handle registration race; stores SHA-256 of token; post-hoc `chmod 0600` pass over pre-existing regular files, fail-open on `IOException`; audit-write failure logged and ignored; `new String(pass)` for JDBC |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/security/RateLimiter.java` | Dual in-memory throttle | Per-source: 5 failures / 5 min → 15 min **block**; per-account: progressive 1–5 s delay, **never denies**; in-memory, single JVM, not persisted |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/service/AuthenticationService.java` | Register / authenticate / validateSession / logout orchestration | Always performs full PBKDF2 (real or dummy); generic error message; slides `expires_at` on every validated request with no absolute cap; deletes session on client-IP mismatch; wipes password buffers |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/validation/InputValidator.java` | Whitelist validation for email, username, password, IP, UUID | Email 5–254 + linear regex; username `^[a-zA-Z0-9_-]{3,32}$` + reserved-name blocklist; password 12–128 + complexity (caps hash-DoS); `validateSourceIp` defaults to `127.0.0.1` and passes `localhost`/`127.0.0.1`/`::1` through unnormalised |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/model/AuthenticationRequest.java` | Login input holder | `char[]` password cloned in constructor, `wipe()`, masked `toString()` |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/model/RegistrationRequest.java` | Registration input holder | Same `char[]` clone / wipe / masked `toString()` |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/model/AuthenticationResult.java` | Login outcome | `GENERIC_FAILURE_MESSAGE = \"Invalid credentials.\"` (no enumeration oracle); `toString()` masks token as `[PROTECTED]` |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/model/RegistrationResult.java` | Registration outcome | Generic user-facing message |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/model/Session.java` | Immutable session record | `requireNonNull` guards; masks `tokenHash` in `toString()` |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/model/SessionCookie.java` | Cookie header builder | Emits `__Host-AUTH_SESSION`; `Path=/; Max-Age; Secure; HttpOnly; SameSite=Strict`; `toString()` masked |\n| `src/main/java/com/security/auth/model/User.java` | User record | Exposes hash/params only to persistence layer; no secrets in `toString()` |\n| `pom.xml` | Build (Java 17, H2 2.3.232, slf4j-simple 2.0.16, JUnit 5.11.4) | `-Xlint:all -Werror`; pinned versions; shade excludes `META-INF/*.SF|DSA|RSA` (signature stripping, benign for an unsigned fat jar) |\n| `README.md` | Design narrative | Asserts 0700/0600, \"constant work\", \"no permissive defaults\", \"no hardcoded secrets\", 59 security tests — several claims contradicted by code/disk (see findings) |\n| `src/test/java/com/security/auth/**Test.java` (6 files) | Unit tests for config, hasher, token generator, rate limiter, service, validator | Test-only; assert the *implemented* behaviour, including that the account throttle returns `allowed=true` |\n\n## 3. Skills Applied\n\nFully loaded and applied (skill bodies read and used to drive the audit):\n\n- **`review-code-for-security-vulnerabilities`** — dispatcher workflow used as the controlling\n  procedure: established what each file actually does first, then matched behaviour to specialised\n  skills by name search, then applied each matched skill body one at a time and reported only\n  findings.\n- **`performing-cryptographic-audit-of-application`** — audited primitives, KDF strength, key\n  material, and randomness: PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA512 parameters and the 100,000-iteration floor\n  (SEC-02), unbounded stored iteration count (SEC-04), the hardcoded dummy secret (SEC-09), and the\n  immutable-`String` passphrase handling (SEC-06). Verified as adequate: platform PBKDF2\n  implementation, 256-bit `SecureRandom` salt, 512-bit derived key, 192-bit session tokens,\n  SHA-256-at-rest for tokens, `MessageDigest.isEqual` comparison.\n- **`testing-api-authentication-weaknesses`** (OWASP API2:2023) — reviewed token entropy,\n  token-in-output leakage, session TTL and revocation-after-logout, and the account-enumeration\n  differential: token printed to stdout (SEC-08), sliding TTL with no absolute cap (SEC-05), the\n  username/password policy battery, and confirmation that timing plus message equalisation close\n  the enumeration oracle.\n- **`exploiting-sql-injection-vulnerabilities`** — attempted to locate string-concatenated SQL in\n  DDL and CRUD paths and considered second-order injection through stored identifiers; all SQL is\n  parameterised (no finding, reported in §6), and the remediation shape in the skill informed the\n  §5 fixes that touch persistence code.\n- **`testing-for-business-logic-vulnerabilities`** — tested workflow-step bypass and race\n  conditions in register → authenticate → validate → logout: the registration TOCTOU handled by\n  UNIQUE constraints plus rollback (§6), and the missing absolute session-lifetime step in the\n  renewal logic (SEC-05).\n- **`implementing-api-schema-validation-security`** — applied its reject-by-default posture to\n  input handling: whitelist username/email/password checks are sound (§6), while the\n  default-on-missing-field behaviour in `validateSourceIp` is the inverse of that principle\n  (SEC-07).\n- **`hunting-credential-stuffing-attacks`** — used for the detection-side reasoning behind the\n  spray analysis (per-account vs per-source telemetry, ASN/velocity signals) that substantiates\n  SEC-03; the skill itself is log/SIEM-oriented, so it contributed analysis rather than code fixes.\n\nReviewed against general OWASP Top 10 / CWE knowledge because **no skill in the library matched**\nthe behaviour in question:\n\n- **File and directory permission hardening** (CWE-276 / CWE-732) — SEC-01. No permission-audit skill\n  exists in the library; assessed against least-privilege file-creation semantics, umask interaction,\n  and TOCTOU windows.\n- **Clear-on-exit / secret lifetime in memory** (CWE-244 / CWE-1188) — SEC-06. Assessed against\n  general JVM secret-handling practice (immutable `String` pooling, zeroing the *master* copy rather\n  than a clone).\n- **Sensitive information written to logs / console output** (CWE-532) — SEC-08.\n- **Insufficient session expiration** (CWE-613) — SEC-05, complementing the authentication skill.\n- **Uncontrolled resource consumption and asymmetric CPU cost** (CWE-400 / CWE-770 / CWE-799) —\n  SEC-03 and SEC-04, including the \"cheap request → expensive server work\" imbalance.\n- **Insufficient logging and monitoring** (CWE-778) — SEC-11 (fail-open audit trail).\n- **Use of a hardcoded value** (CWE-798) — SEC-09's dummy literal, judged on its actual\n  (non-authenticating) role.\n- **Cleartext transmission of the credential to a terminal** (CWE-522 / CWE-359) — SEC-10's echoing\n  stdin fallback.\n\nFrontmatter-only candidates that were checked and deliberately **not** applied, as their behaviour\ndoes not occur in this codebase: `testing-for-broken-access-control` (no HTTP authorization surface;\nauthorization is single-tenant CLI), `performing-hash-cracking-with-hashcat` (used only to reason\nabout offline crackability of a world-readable store, not as an audit procedure),\n`implementing-secret-scanning-with-gitleaks` (no committed secrets; SEC-09 is a code literal, not a\ncredential).\n\n## 4. Summary of Findings\n\n| ID | Title | Severity | CWE | Location |\n|---|---|---|---|---|\n| SEC-01 | Credential store delivered world-readable; permission enforcement is post-hoc, leaf-only and fails open | High | CWE-732 | `src/main/java/com/security/auth/config/SecurityConfig.java:117` |\n| SEC-02 | PBKDF2 iteration floor of 100,000 permits downgrade below the code's own default | Medium | CWE-326 | `src/main/java/com/security/auth/crypto/PasswordHasher.java:35` |\n| SEC-03 | Account rate-limit dimension never denies, enabling unbounded distributed password spraying | Medium | CWE-799 | `src/main/java/com/security/auth/security/RateLimiter.java:80` |\n| SEC-04 | Attacker-influenced iteration count used without an upper bound during verification | Medium | CWE-770 | `src/main/java/com/security/auth/crypto/PasswordHasher.java:155` |\n| SEC-05 | Session expiry slides forever with no absolute lifetime cap | Medium | CWE-613 | `src/main/java/com/security/auth/service/AuthenticationService.java:297` |\n| SEC-06 | Database passphrase materialised as immutable `String`;
